中国地质 · 华北克拉通与岩石圈减薄（含山东）Geology of China

中国地质（第 3 部分，共 5 部分）：三、华北克拉通与岩石圈减薄（含山东）

三、华北克拉通与岩石圈减薄（含山东）

本章共 3 篇（下详）

Continental crust of China: A brief guide for the perplexed（中国大陆地壳：解惑简明指南）

Stern RJ, 闵志斌, 等. 2018. Earth-Science Reviews（英文全文照录） · 原文：https://personal.utdallas.edu/~rjstern/pdfs/SternChinaESR18.pdf

提要：国外权威综述：中国三大前寒武纪克拉通（华北/扬子/塔里木）与显生宙造山带拼合；【含山东】华北克拉通东部（鲁西/胶东）基底 ~3.8-2.5 Ga 演化与中生代克拉通破坏

Contents lists available at ScienceDirectEarth-Science Reviews journal homepage: www.elsevier.com/locate/earscirevContinental crust of China: A brief guide for the perplexedRobert J.Sterna,⁎, Shi-Min Lib, G.Randy Kellerca Geosciences Department, University of Texas at Dallas, Richardson, TX 75080, USAb State Key Laboratory of Geological Processes and Mineral Resources, and School of Earth Science and Resources, China University of Geosciences, Beijing 100083, Chinac School of Geology and Geophysics, The University of Oklahoma, Norman, OK, USAA R T I C L E I N F O Keywords: Continental crust China Continental growth Tethys A B S T R A C T China covers approximately 10 million km2 and its crust has a complicated evolution of amalgamation, igneousactivity, and sedimentation.Many studies address various aspects of China's crust, but few provide a simplegeological and geophysical overview that is accessible to students and non-specialists; Filling this void is theobjective of this review.

China is characterized by thick (40–75 km) crust in the west due to Cenozoic collisionwith India and thin (30–40 km thick) crust in the east due to E–W Mesozoic-Cenozoic backarc extension.Incontrast, overall crustal fabric trends E–W, deﬁned by ophiolite belts and ultra-high pressure metamorphic rocks.This crustal fabric indicates that China has grown like a sandwich, with crust progressively added throughPhanerozoic time by closing various E–W oriented Tethys oceans and seaways.In map view, China consists ofﬁve E–W trending tiers.Tier 1 is deﬁned by the Central Asian Orogenic Belt (CAOB) along the northern margin of China, whichconsists of the Xing'an-Mongolia orogenic belt in the NE and the Tianshan Orogen in the NW.The CAOB formedduring ~1000 Ma to ~250 Ma and is an accretionary orogen of mostly Paleozoic age that formed throughclosure of the Paleo-Asian Ocean and collision between the Siberian Craton and Archean-Paleoproterozoic crustto the south, which constitutes Tier 2.The CAOB has a strong aeromagnetic signature.

Sediments from the AmurRiver show detrital U-Pb zircon age peaks at 2.8–2.3 Ga, 1.8 Ga, 450–250 Ma, and 200–100 Ma, which is ex-pected for erosion of the Xing'an-Mongolia belt.Tier 1 igneous rocks are mainly Paleozoic except in the NE(Xing'an-Mongolia orogenic belt) and reﬂect subduction of the Paleo-Asian Ocean and associated accretionevents, whereas Paleozoic CAOB crust in the east is overprinted by Jurassic and Cretaceous igneous rocks relatedto subduction of ancient Paciﬁc basin oceanic lithosphere.Tier 2 includes the North China Craton (NCC) to the east and Tarim Craton to the west.The NCC contains theoldest rocks in China and is dominated by Archean and Paleoproterozoic ages.The extent of Archean rocks in theNCC may have been overestimated, as suggested by detrital zircons from the Yellow River, which ﬂows acrossthe craton, showing age peaks at 2.5–2.2 Ga, ~1.9 Ga, 500–400 Ma, and 300–200 Ma.

The Tarim Craton isdominated by Palaeoproterozoic- Mesoproterozoic metamorphic strata along with a signiﬁcant proportion ofNeoproterozoic (~0.8 Ga) rocks.U-Pb ages for detrital zircons from Tarim River sediments reﬂect this basementgeology, with strong peaks of Early and Late Paleozoic age, less abundant Neoproterozoic ages, and scatteredages back to the Archean.The NCC also was aﬀected by abundant Mesozoic igneous activity with voluminousEarly Cretaceous rocks that are associated with lithospheric thinning and decratonization.Tier 3 – also known as the Central China Orogen - is composed of the Sulu-Dabie-Qinling-Kunlun Orogen andrecords closing of an arm of Prototethys during the Ordovician to Silurian and Paleotethys during the Triassic.Tier 3 contains one of Earth's three giant ultra-high pressure (UHP) terranes with well-documented peak me-tamorphism of 650–850 °C and 4 GPa, indicating that some of these rocks were deeply subducted and thenexhumed from depths of over 120 km in Triassic time.

Tier 3 magmatism occurred in two episodes, early-middlePaleozoic and Triassic.Tier 4 contains blocks rifted from Gondwana, which include the Songpan-Ganzi, Qiangtang, and Lhasa ter-ranes of Tibet in the west and the South China Block in the east.These terranes are marked by broad magneticanomalies with a NE-SW trend along the Paciﬁc margin, and a broad N–S trending anomaly between Tibet andSouth China.The South China Block is made up of Proterozoic and minor Archean crust of the Yangtze andCathaysia blocks, which collided at 1.0–0.8 Ga to form the Jiangnan Orogen and the South China Block.Agespectra for detrital zircons from the Yangtze and Pearl Rivers shows major peaks at ~1.8 Ga, 900–800 Ma,~400 Ma, and 300–150 Ma, which is consistent with the age of South China Block crust.Early-Middle Paleozoichttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.earscirev.2018.01.020Received 15 October 2017; Received in revised form 23 January 2018; Accepted 31 January 2018⁎ Corresponding author.E-mail address: ［邮箱略］ (R.J.Stern).

Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–94Available online 15 February 20180012-8252/ 2018 Published by Elsevier B.V.T igneous rocks are also found in South China.Mesozoic igneous rocks are widespread in both South China andTibet and are related to subduction of the Paleo-Paciﬁc and Tethyan oceanic plates, respectively.The accretionof Tibetan terranes to southern Eurasia occurred in the Mesozoic before collision with India at ~55 Ma.Tier 5 is represented by the island of Taiwan on the SE margin of China and marks where China crustcontinues to grow.Taiwan lies on a complex convergent boundary between the South China Block to the NW, thePhilippine Sea Plate to the SE, and the Sunda Plate to the SW. 1.Introduction Many geoscientists would like to better understand the crust ofChina.This is not easy because the country covers almost 10 mil-lion km2, the size of a small continent.This immense region has acomplicated tectonic evolution, encompassing most of Earth's history.

This challenge is compounded because the geoscientiﬁc literature onChina is immense and increasingly specialized, making it diﬃcult forbeginners and non-experts to grasp the key features and episodes.Thepurpose of this paper is to brieﬂy review the main elements of China'scrust.It is aimed at advanced undergraduate and beginning graduatestudents and experts in other ﬁelds, such as geophysicists, igneouspetrologists studying Cenozoic igneous rocks, economic geologists, andgeologists of other nations.Experts in China's geology and geophysicsare likely to notice that many important contributions are omitted, butsuch omissions cannot be avoided in an overview paper like this.Below, we brieﬂy summarize how crustal growth and crustalstructure in China and Eurasia diﬀers from that of the USA and NorthAmerica, and how these two continents and countries represent alter-nate styles of continent formation.Then we outline the geology ofChina's crust, simplifying it into ﬁve E–W trending strips of lithosphereor “tiers”.

We summarize the Phanerozoic igneous rocks of China,outline the several important sedimentary basins of China, and examinewhat detrital zircons from ﬁve great rivers of China reveal about thecrust.Finally, we provide a geophysical overview from both potentialﬁelds (gravity and magnetic) and active-source seismology, includingrepresentative seismic lines that reveal large-scale features of China'scrust at depth.Our goal for this simple overview is to help students andresearchers from other ﬁelds grasp and understand the most importantaspects of China's complex crust. 2.Diﬀerent ways of making a continent: Eurasia (China) andNorth America (USA) Two of the six continents continue to grow.The four southerncontinents, i.e., South America, Africa, Australia, and Antarctica, arerifted fragments of Gondwana.These have not grown since they formedin Mesozoic and Cenozoic time when Gondwana broke up.The othertwo continents – North America and Eurasia – have grown signiﬁcantlyduring Mesozoic and Cenozoic time.

These two continents diﬀer in howthey grew.The basic geologic structure of Eurasia (as captured byChina) diﬀers fundamentally from that of North America, formerly thecrust of the ancient continent of Laurentia (as represented today by theUnited States of America), as summarized in Fig. 1.It is especiallyuseful to compare how the crust of these two nations formed becausethey are about the same size in area: 9.6 × 106 km2 for China; 9.8 × 106 km2 for the United States.North America consists of an Archean core, exposed as the CanadianShield, which is surrounded by younger crustal tracts that were addedin Paleoproterozoic-Mesoproterozoic,Paleozoic, Mesozoic, and Cenozoic time.During the ﬁrst mega-episode, between 1800 Ma and1000 Ma, the Yavapai, Mazatzal, Granite-Rhyolite Province, and Grenville terranes were added to the SE margin of the Archean craton(Whitmeyer and Karlstrom, 2007).During the second mega-episode(Paleozoic, ~541–251 Ma), Taconic, Acadian, and Allegheny-Ouachita-Marathon crust was added in the east and south.

During the thirdepisode (Mesozoic, 251–66 Ma), oceanic terranes and crust was addedto the west and in Alaska.The ﬁnal episode (Paleogene, 66–23 Ma)witnessed crustal growth in the Paciﬁc NW by accretion of Siletzia andin the SE by accretion of Cuba.Crustal growth by addition of youngercrust around an ancient core was somewhat like the growth rings of atree trunk and has continued semi-continuously for the past 1800 mil-lion years.Eurasian continental growth is quite diﬀerent.Eurasia is the largestcontinent and continues to grow.Whereas North America has grownmore like a tree trunk, Eurasia has grown like a sandwich, with newslices added to the bottom.Eurasia is anchored by twin northern cra-tons: the Baltic craton in the NW and the Siberian craton in the NE.Crust has been added to the southern margins of these nuclei, whichhave been fused to form a single continental platform in the Paleozoic.

Eurasia (and China) grew by closing various Tethyan oceans, includingPrototethys, Paleotethys, Mesotethys, Neotethys, beginning about1 billion years ago and continuing into the future with closing of theIndian Ocean.Each time a new Tethys seaway opened, it sliced piecesof Greater Gondwana away and ultimately plastered them along thesouth side of Eurasia along with various oceanic fragments and sedi-ments picked up during northward migration.The growth of Eurasiacontinues today with the addition of Taiwan, India, Arabia, and even-tually Australia.Another big diﬀerence between the crust of the two nations is thatUSA crust is never > 50 km thick, whereas crust in western China isleast 50 km thick and can be up to 75 km thick beneath Tibet.Anotherbig diﬀerence is the presence of ophiolite belts as an integral part ofChina's crustal fabric, whereas North America only has ophiolites on itsmargins.

This indicates that during the Phanerozoic evolution of Chinaseveral oceans had to close and were accompanied by major collisionalorogenies.Such collisions are also identiﬁed by the abundance of ultra-high pressure (UHP) metamorphic rocks containing metamorphic coe-site or diamond, which indicate that subduction to depths of at least100 km occurred.In contrast, UHP metamorphic rocks are not known tooccur in North America. 3.The ﬁve tiers of ChinaIn some ways, the crustal conﬁguration of China is simple.In mapview, China consists of ﬁve broad strips that trend E–W, which we referto as tiers (Fig. 2).Each tier has a distinct tectonic evolution.Tier 1 isnorthernmost and is part of the Central Asian Orogenic Belt (CAOB)which traverses Asia from the Paciﬁc Ocean to the Ural Mountains.TheCAOB in China is > 4000 km long and > 1000 km wide.It is the closedtrace of Prototethys, sometimes called the Paleo-Asian Ocean, and issandwiched between the Siberian craton to the north and Tier 2 to thesouth.

The lithosphere of Tier 2 is Archean and Paleoproterozoic in age.Tier 2 consists of the large triangular North China Craton (NCC) to theeast, the smaller Tarim Craton to the west, and a narrow strip of Ar-chean-Paleoproterozoic crust which connects the two.South of theArchean-Paleoproterozoic belt, Tier 3 marks a major Triassic suture,which is known as the Sulu-Dabie-Qinling-Qilian-Kunlun orogen.Tier 3marks the closed trace of Paleotethys, where rifted blocks from Gond-wana collided with growing Eurasia.Orogenesis in Tier 3 was parti-cularly intense, with several regions subducted to at least 150 km beforebeing brought back to the surface.Tier 4 consists of South China andTibet, and has an especially diverse geology from east to west.To thewest in Tibet, three terranes are present: the Songpan-Ganzi, R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9473 Table 1 Compilation of age data of ophiolites in China.

China(and Eurasia) has grown like a sandwich, with crustal tracts progressively added on from the south through Phanerozoic time whereas the USA (and North America) has grown like a treetrunk, with crust added to a Precambrian core, ﬁrst on the eastern and southern in Paleozoic time, then on the western and southern during Mesozoic and younger time.R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9475 3.1.Tier one: The Central Asian Orogenic BeltThe Central Asian Orogenic Belt (CAOB) records an important epi-sode of crustal growth and reworking during the Neoproterozoic andPaleozoic.The CAOB is sometimes referred to as the Altaids; the CAOBin China is the easternmost part of the orogen.The CAOB formed at thesame time as the Caledonian-Hercynian Orogen in western Europe andthe Allegheny-Ouachita-Marathon Orogen in North America and theTerra Australis Orogen along the southern margin of Gondwana.

Theseevents reﬂect the opening and closing of the Prototethys, culminating incollision of Gondwana with Laurussia to form the supercontinentPangea.Prototethys in Asia is sometimes called the Paleo-Asian Ocean.Because the CAOB is long and wide, it has many subdivisions andnames.In China, the northeastern part is sometimes called the Xing'an-Mongolia orogenic belt.In NW China, these rocks are sometimes re-ferred to as the Junggar segment and the Tianshan Orogen, whichcontinues eastward into the Beishan Orogen.Several blocks or terraneshave been identiﬁed in the NE; from E to NW these are the N–S trendingKhanka-Jiamusui Block, the Songliao Block, the Xing'an Block, and theErguna blocks (Zhou and Wilde, 2013).Other names for various blocksand terranes are given by diﬀerent authors, but the basis for furthersubdivision is often unclear.The CAOB is taken as a type example of an accretionary orogen(Cawood et al., 2009).

Accretionary orogens form by additing bothjuvenile arc and oceanic plateau crust as well as continental slivers tothe margin of a continent, in a manner comparable with that of manycircum-Paciﬁc Mesozoic-Cenozoic accretionary orogens, including thewestern USA.Accretionary orogens contrast with collisional orogens inthat the latter involve collision of a large continental block, such as thecollision of India with Asia.The CAOB formed from ~1000 Ma to ~250 Ma by accretion ofophiolites, oceanic islands, seamounts, accretionary wedges, oceanicplateaux, and microcontinents (Fig. 3).Final closure of the Prototethys/Paleo-Asian Ocean occurred during the Permian and formed the So-lonker Suture (Windley et al., 2007).Whereas most of the ~750 Mahistory of the CAOB is that of an accretionary orogen, its ﬁnal stagesinvolved continental collision with Tier 2 continental crust.Our thinking about CAOB accretion events and the nature of theterranes involved continues to evolve.

Early workers (Sengör et al.,1993) thought that the CAOB represented a single long-lived con-vergent margin, and that an unusually large amount of new continentalcrust formed (B.M.Jahn et al., 2004).It now appears that several arc-backarc basin systems of various ages were involved and that half of theCAOB is composed of reworked older continental crust (Kröner et al.,2014).Recent studies emphasize the presence of ~500 Ma crust andargue that collision continued into the Triassic (Zhou and Wilde, 2013).An interesting question that is just starting to be addressed is the extentto which Late Ediacaran-Cambrian crust (~600–500 Ma) (known asCadomian in Europe) is present in the CAOB.An important tectonic transition occurred in the Chinese CAOB atabout the Carboniferous-Permian boundary (~300 Ma).Before thistime, the igneous rocks are I-type granitic plutons and arc-like volcanicsequences and are associated with volcaniclastic and ﬂysch deposits; these rocks are similar to those of modern convergent plate margins.

After this time, the igneous rocks change to A-type plutons and molasse-type terrestrial sediments, as expected for post-collisional tectonic en-vironments.The Permian is also when plume activity began and hugevolumes of basalt ﬂooded the surrounding regions (Siberian Traps,Emeishan Large Igneous Province (LIP), Tarim LIP).Permian anoro-genic plutons aﬀected broad regions of the CAOB and these were in-truded about the same time that the great mantle plumes generated LIPsto the north, west, and south.Subsequently, extension occurred duringthe Triassic and the easternmost CAOB was punctured in the Jurassicand Cretaceous by intermediate and felsic plutons related to subductionof the Paleo-Paciﬁc plate.The Mongol-Okhotsk Orogen, located to the north of Xing'an-Mongolia orogenic belt in China, is an interesting exception to thesimpliﬁed story of the CAOB discussed above.Evidence of the Mongol-Okhotsk Ocean is preserved in and around the Mongol-Okhotsk suturebelt, which trends NE from Mongolia into easternmost Russia.

Relatedigneous rocks are found in the Erguna Massif (Fig. 2) of northeastern-most China (Li et al., 2017a, 2017b, 2017c).

The Mongol-OkhotskOcean began as the easternmost part of the CAOB but remained openinto Jurassic time (Donskaya et al., 2013), long after the Paleo-AsianOcean closed to form the CAOB farther west. 0 500 1000 km 70°E 80°E 90°E 100°E 110°E 120°E 130°E 140°E 40°N 30°N 40°N 30°N 20°N 100°E 110°E 120°E 90°E South China Craton Cathaysia Foldbelt Taiwan Hainan Yangtze Craton Himalaya Orogen Jiangnan Orogen North Lhasa Yidun Indochina Western Qiangtang Songpan-Ganzi Dabie Tan-Lu Fault Sulu Western Block Songliao Xing’an Erguna Eastern Block Trans-North China Orogen Ogcheon Imjingang Mongolia Kazakhstan India Craton 0 500 1000 km North China Craton Tier 1 Tier 1 Tier 2 Tier 2 Tier 1 Tier 3 Tier 4 Tier 4 Tier 5 Eastern Qiangtang Central Lhasa South Lhasa Central Orogenic Asia Belt 100°E 110°E 120°E 90°E Longmenshan Tianshan Tarim Craton Tarim LIP Emeishan LIP Junggar Precambrian cratonic blockLarge Igneous Province Phanerozoic orogen Suture zone/Major fault Sibumasu W-Burma Altaids Qinling Beishan Comei LIP Jiamusui East-Kunlun Qilian Qaidam Tier 3 Korean Vietnam Peninsula Fig. 2.

Simpliﬁed tectonic map of China, after Y.F.Zheng et al.(2013).China has 3 signiﬁcant large igneous provinces (LIPs), the Emeishan of Permian age (Shellnutt, 2014), the Tarimwith 3 episodes at 300, 290, and 280 Ma (Y.G.Xu et al., 2014), and the Early Cretaceous (132 Ma) Comei LIP (D.C.Zhu et al., 2009).R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9476 3.2.Tier two: Ancient continental nucleiTier 2 is made up of the oldest rocks in China.Archean rocks inChina are mostly found in Tier 2 and are mostly (but not exclusively)from the North China Craton (NCC).The NCC is composed of Archeanand Paleoproterozoic basement.Sometimes this is called the Sino-Korean craton.The oldest rocks in China – or in Asia – are found in theAnshan region in the Eastern Block of the NCC, where ~3.8 Gatrondhjemites, orthogneiss, and migmatite are documented (W.Wanet al., 2012).The NCC is divided into two Archean blocks separated byPaleoproterozoic mobile belts (Zhao and Cawood, 2012).

Paleoproterozoic metamorphism reached amphibolite and granuliteconditions.The Western Block is the most stable part of the craton.TheEastern Block was thinned due to extension that began in Mesozoictime.Destabilization of this craton likely reﬂected weakening by hy-dration from below, due to water released by the subducted Paleo-Pa-ciﬁc, Izanagi and Paciﬁc plates.The Tarim Craton deﬁnes the western side of Tier 2 and is domi-nated by Palaeoproterozoic high-grade metamorphic rocks and Mesoproterozoic lower-grade metamorphic strata.

There are minorNeo-Archean tonalite-trondhjemite-granodiorite(TTG) gneisses, Paleoproterozoic medium- to high-grade metamorphic rocks, andMesoproterozoic-Neoproterozoic greenschist facies metamorphic rocks0 500 1000 km 70°E 80°E 90°E 100°E 110°E 120°E 130°E 140°E 40°N 40°N 30°N 20°N 100°E 110°E 120°E 90°E 122 130-123 138 130-118 166 182 163 165 167 345 438 188 240 308 332 516 509 501 534-517 500 518 449 483-457 490-479 550-529 419 440 471 483 515-484 236 292 264 282 444 267 178 150 178 320 300 163 467 399392 425 439 444-410 356 880 848-820 3.7-3.5 10-1 172 376 126 119 115 993 332 344-325 572 531-516 414-395 426-388 403 307 493 519 478 472 516 425 420 291-272 279 280 256 274-253 297 241 304-295 354-329 318-298 Sulu: 242-214 Altyn: 509-475 West Tianshan: Qaidam: 457-420 Locations and ages of ophiolites (Ma)Cretaceous 130 Locations and ages of UHP rocks (Ma)Dabie: 220 Neogene Jurassic Triassic Permian Carboniferous Devonian Silurian Ordovician Cambrian Neoproterozoic 190 1027-1014 344 305 218 354-345 26 25 24 23 22 21 12 11 10 9 20 19 18 17 16 14 15 13 8 7 5 6 4 3 2 1 Ophiolites (B) (A) Number 0 4 8 12 Neo-Proterozoic Mesozoic Paleozoic 0 200 400 600 800 1000 1200 metamorphic rocks (C) Age (Ma) 0 200 400 600 800 1000 1200 Age (Ma) 0 2 1 3 Neo-Proterozoic Mesozoic Paleozoic UHP 321-305, 233-226 Dabie: 240-220 Qinling: 490? (A) Ophiolites and ultra-high pressure terranes of China.Ophiolites (colored circles and triangles) and Ultra-High Pressure (UHP) metamorphic terranes of China (stars).Ages inMa.B) Histogram of ophiolite ages summarized in Table 1.C) Histogram of UHP ages from Liou et al., 2009 (Dabie-Sulu); H.Wang et al., 2014 (Qinling); Chen et al., 2009a, 2009b(Qilian); Liu et al., 2009a, 2009b (Altyn); Li et al., 2016a (SW Tianshan); M.Sang et al., 2016 (Atbashi); and M.L.Leech et al.(2005) (Tso Morari) Precambrian age ranges for crustalblocks from Zhai and Zhou (2015).R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9477 (X.Long et al., 2010).The Eastern Kunlun-Qaidam-Qilian Orogen is composed of Paleoproterozoic basement (2.4–1.8 Ga) and Neoproterozoic to EarlyPaleozoic cover (F.Liu et al., 2015).The understanding of the Pre-cambrian geology of this orogen is still very limited.An interesting complication is that the Tarim Craton and NCC showdiﬀerent U-Pb zircon age distributions.

The NCC is dominated byArchean and Paleoproterozoic ages, with two major peaks at 2.5 Ga and1.8 Ga (Rojas-Agramonte et al., 2011).This is very similar to the agedistribution of the Siberian Craton, which also shows major peaks at2.6 Ga and 1.9 Ga.The NCC and Siberian Craton may have rifted apart.In contrast, the Tarim Craton is dominated by Neoproterozoic (~0.8 Ga) ages, consistent with an origin from Greater Gondwana(Rojas-Agramonte et al., 2011). 3.3.Tier three: Sulu-Dabie-Qinling-Qilian-Kunlun OrogenThis intensely deformed zone is also referred to as the Central ChinaOrogen.Two sutures are identiﬁed as the sites of ancient oceans inDabie-Qinling segment, the Early Paleozoic Shang Dan (Prototethys,with Early Paleozoic ophiolites) and the Late Paleozoic-TriassicMianlue ocean (Paleotethys; Dong et al., 2011a, 2011b).The QinlingOrogen contains abundant Tonian igneous rocks (Zhang et al., 2016a,2016b, 2016c) and is punctured by plutons of Early Paleozoic, LateTriassic, and Late Jurassic–Early Cretaceous age.

Triassic orogeny wasespecially intense, as demonstrated by abundant ultrahigh pressuremetamorphic rocks (UHP terranes).Tier 3 contains one of Earth's greatexpanses of UHP terranes (Fig. 3).UHP rocks cover an area exceeding10,000 km2, with a well-documented peak metamorphism of 650–850 °C and 4 GPa.These results indicate that some of these rockswere subducted to and then exhumed from at least 135 km deep in theEarth (Hacker et al., 2006).Farther west in the Qilian-Kunlun segment, the Paleotethys sutureshows similar characteristics (Dong et al., 2011a, 2011b).The Qaidamand Qilian micro-continental blocks occur to the north of the EastKunlun Orogen, and are dominated by Early Paleozoic plutons.Crustalformation of the Qilian segment records closure of an arm of Proto-tethys, i.e., Qilian Ocean, which is represented by the Early PaleozoicNorth Qilian belt and North Qaidam UHP belt (S.Song et al., 2013).There is good evidence for an Early Paleozoic orogeny in easternKunlun (Dong et al., 2011a, 2011b).

The East Kunlun Orogen (EKO) isinterpreted as a collage of terranes that accumulated from south tonorth over time.The North Kunlun, Middle Kunlun, and South Kunlunfaults divide the EKO into the Northeastern Kunlun back-arc basin belt,the Middle Eastern Kunlun island arc belt, and the Southeastern KunlunPaleo-oceanic basin.The EKO shows evidence of four episodes of ig-neous activity: Proterozoic, Early Paleozoic, Permian to Early Jurassic,and post-early Jurassic.Eclogites in the central belt show Late Ordo-vician to Early Devonian U-Pb zircon ages. 3.4.Tier four: Proterozoic rocks of South China and the composite Lhasa-Qiangtang terrane of TibetTier 4 crustal blocks were rifted from Gondwana and accreted toAsia.The South China Block itself is composite, consisting of theYangtze Block in the north and the Cathaysia Block in the south, se-parated by the 1500 km-long Jiangnan or Sibao Orogen (Fig. 2).

TheYangtze Block contains minor Paleoproterozoic rocks and abundantMesoproterozoic crust whereas the Cathaysia Block contains signiﬁcantMesoproterozoic and abundant Neoproterozoic crust (Zhai and Zhou,2015).The rocks of the Jiangnan Orogen consist of Neoproterozoic(Tonian) sequences of clastic sediments and maﬁc-ultramaﬁc igneousrocks (J.G.Zhao et al., 2011; P.A.Cawood et al., 2017).The tectonicsetting in which these sequences formed is controversial, ranging fromcontinental rift to convergent margin to backarc basin.In South China,Cryogenian and Ediacaran sequences form sedimentary cover, in whichglacial deposits are well preserved.These sedimentary rocks are oftencalled “Sinian” in China.Late Mesoproterozoic to Middle Neoproter-ozoic tectono-thermal events can be subdivided into two major periods.The ﬁrst period (1.05–0.90 Ga) helped form the Yangtze Craton andreﬂects a compressional regime corresponding to the ﬁnal assembly ofRodinia.

The second period (0.82–0.74 Ga) may reﬂect rifting related tobreak-up of the Rodinia supercontinent (S.Lu et al., 2008).The Songpan-Ganzi Terrane is a massive ﬂysch complex betweenSouth China and Tibet.This terrane extends from the western margin ofSouth China to the Pamirs over a distance of ~2000 km and is perhapsthe least understood tectonic unit in Tibet.Like Tier 3 rocks, this ter-rane experienced intense Triassic deformation and also shares manyfeatures in common with Qiangtang and Qaidam terranes (K.J.Zhang,2001).It consists of Precambrian basement that is covered by thickTriassic ﬂysch, all thrusted to the NE.Triassic ﬂysch is associated with astretched Cambro-Ordovician arc in the southern Kunlun-Qaidam Ter-rane in eastern Tibet.Here, the Triassic ﬂysch rests on top of Permian orDevonian strata, which are part of an 8–9 km thick Neoproterozoic-Early Paleozoic passive margin sequence overlying the South Chinabasement.

In the westernmost Songpan-Ganzi Terrane, meta-volcanicbasement rocks yield a U-Pb zircon age of 2481 ± 14 Ma.The presenceof Neoarchean-Paleoproterozoic basement and the overlying Neopro-terozoic sequence suggest that the Songpan-Ganzi Terrane has beenpart of South China since Neoproterozoic time (K.J.Zhang, 2001).Tibet south of the Songpan-Ganzi Terrane consists of the compositeQiangtang Terrane in the north and the composite Lhasa Terrane in thesouth (Yin and Harrison, 2000; Zhu et al., 2013).The Qiangtang Ter-rane accreted to the Songpan-Ganzi Terrane along the Longmu Tso –Shuanghu – Lancangjiang suture zone, which marks where an arm ofPaleotethys once existed.Rocks of the suture zone include Paleozoicophiolites, arc volcanic rocks, and high-pressure metamorphic rocks(Fig. 3).This seaway opened in Late Ediacaran time and closed in theTriassic (Xie et al., 2017).

The southern (sometimes called eastern) Qiangtang subterranecontains an Ordovician and older crystalline basement (Pullen et al.,2011) and mainly Late Paleozoic and Triassic – Jurassic cover, whereasthe oldest rocks exposed in the northern (sometimes called western)Qiangtang subterrane are Devonian, which are overlain by sediments asyoung as Jurassic (D.C.Zhu et al., 2013).The northern and southernQiangtang subterranes coalesced during the Triassic (Q.G.Zhai et al.,2011).The composite Qiangtang Terrane provided a strong lithosphericblock on the south side of Eurasia against which the Lhasa Terraneaccreted, forming the Bangong-Nujiang suture zone (D.C.Zhu et al.,2016).Bangong-Nujiang suture rocks include Jurassic shale and coarserclastic sediments, mélange, ophiolites and Jurassic-Cretaceous igneousrocks.The Lhasa Terrane is built on Neoproterozoic-Cambrian crust, ran-ging in age from 520 to 460 Ma; this is may be an eastern extension ofCadomian crust known in Anatolia-Iran and western Europe.

Somegneisses as old as Paleoproterozoic are also reported (Y.H.Lin et al.,2013).Lhasa Terrane can be divided into southern, central, andnorthern subterranes.Archean basement is preserved in its centralsubterrane whereas crust in the southern and northern segments islikely younger than Mesoproterozoic based on zircon Hf isotope map-ping (Zhu et al., 2011).The southern limit of the Lhasa Terrane is theIndus-Yarlung Zangbo suture, which marks the former site of Neo-tethys, closed by the collision between India and Eurasia starting about55 Ma (D.C.Zhu et al., 2015, 2017b).The Sanjiang region (Fig.3), lying between South China Block andTibetan Plateau, also contains terranes rifted from Gondwana, in-cluding the northern part of the Indochina (Simao block) and thenorthern part of the Sibumasu (Baoshan block) (J.Deng et al., 2014), aswell as the Tengchong Terrane, which can be linked to the Lhasa Ter-rane in Tibet (e.g., J.Xie et al., 2016).R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9478 3.5.

Tier ﬁve: Cenozoic foldbelt of TaiwanTier 5 is a region of active crustal growth on the SE margin of China,represented by the island of Taiwan.Taiwan lies on a complex con-vergent boundary between the South China Block to the west and north,the Philippine Sea Plate to the east and south, and the Sunda Plate tothe southwest.Taiwan exists because thick sediments of China's passivecontinental margin are subducted beneath the Philippine Sea plate.Thepassive margin, east of China, is converging with the Philippine Seaplate at 7 cm per year in a NW-SE direction (W.N.Wu et al., 2009).Thishas compressed Cenozoic sediments, folding and faulting them intomountainous relief, which is rising at a rate of several mm per year (Foxet al., 2014).The geology of Taiwan can be subdivided into two longitudinalstrips, the Coastal Range Terrane along the SE coast and the CentralRange Terrane (C.S.Ho, 1986).The Coastal Range terrane is part thePhilippine Sea Plate.

West of this is the Eastern Central Range Terrane,part of the upturned China continental shelf which is partly subductedunderneath the Luzon Arc.The oldest exposed rocks are strongly me-tamorphosed and found in the Central Mountains.

Poor quality fossilevidence suggests a Permian origin for these sediments. 0 500 1000 km 110°E 130°E 140°E 40°N 30°N 40°N 30°N 20°N 100°E 110°E 120°E 90°E Mongolia India Craton 0 500 1000 km 100°E 110°E 120°E 90°E 90-70 190-180 180-170 200-170 265-250 220-195 Yidun, 230-210 250-220 170-105 120-10 230-210 260-210 250-180 150-100 220-175 185-160 250-170 136-115 190-150 255-247 140-80 190-150 250-200 250-185 160-100 140-110 ~160 260-200 130-120 70-40 K-E J T K-E J T Granitoids Volcanic rocks Cenozoic basalts T-J (b) (A) Paleozoic (541 - 252 Ma)(B) Mesozoic & Cenozoic (252 Ma - present)240-220 Altyn Tagh 0 500 1000 km 100°E 110°E 140°E 40°N 30°N 40°N 30°N 20°N 100°E 110°E 120°E 90°E Mongolia India Craton 0 500 1000 km 100°E 110°E 120°E 90°E Emeishan LIP ~260 ~290 Tarim LIP 480-430 520-430 490-370 360-250 360-250 530-480 460-400 500-270 430, 340-290 336-307 C-P Granitoids O-D Є-O Granite Complexes Large Igneous Provinces 330-260 540-430 Comei LIP ~132 Ma Keramay Dulate-Baytag & Yemaquan Yinshan Yanshan Liaobei-Yanbian Liaodong Jiaodong Korean Peninsula Taiwan Hainan Altyn Tagh Bachu Uplift Taiwan Fig. 4.

Sketch map showing the distribution of Phanerozoic magmatic rocks of China and Korea.A)Paleozoic, B) Mesozoic-Cenozoic.The numbers are theages of the main magmatism stage in Ma.Distributionand age data are from F.Y.Wu et al.(2011) fornortheastern China; S.W.Kim et al.(2015), M.G.Zhaiet al.(2016) for Korea; Zhang et al.(2014a, 2014b,2014c) for North China; Wang et al.(2013a, 2013b,2013d, 2013c) for Qinling; Zhao and Zheng (2009) forDabie-Sulu; Y.Wang et al.(2013a) and Li et al.(2017a, 2017b, 2017c) for South China; J.Yin et al.(2017) for NW Junggar; Y.Xiao et al.(2011) for eastJunggar; L.Long et al.(2011) for Tian Shan; Huanget al.(2015a, 2015b), H.Yang et al.(2016) for Qilian; X.Chen et al.(2015) for Qaidam; Zhang et al.(2014a,2014b, 2014c) for Songpan-Ganzi; L.Lu et al.(2017)for Qiangtang; D.C.Zhu et al.(2013, 2017) for Lhasa; Wang et al.(2016a, 2016b) for Sanjiang; Z.Chu et al.(2017) for Cenozoic basalts; Shellnutt (2014) forEmeishan LIP, Y.G.Xu et al.(2014) for Tarim LIP, D.C.Zhu et al.(2009) for Comei LIP.R.J.

Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9479 4.Phanerozoic igneous rocksIgneous activity at convergent plate margins before terrane accre-tion, during collision, and after orogeny resulted in signiﬁcant growthof China's crust.Plutons intruded the crust during the Paleozoic andMesozoic and ﬂood basalt volcanism associated with Permian andCretaceous mantle plumes also contributed to China's crust.Alkali ba-salts erupted in eastern China during the Cenozoic as well (Fig. 4). 4.1.Paleozoic igneous rocksPaleozoic igneous rocks occur in the CAOB (Tier 1), Qinling-Qilian-Kunlun Orogen (Tier 3), and Cathaysia Block of South China (Tier 4)(Fig. 4A).Magmatism in the CAOB occurred from Cambrian to Permiantime, reﬂecting a long accretion history.In the Xing'an-Mongolia orogenic belt of the CAOB in NE China,Paleozoic igneous rocks are scattered among more abundant Mesozoicplutons, so their spatial signiﬁcance remains unclear (F.Y.Wu et al.,2011).

Available data suggest that Paleozoic magmatism began at540 Ma and lasted until 230 Ma, with major episodes at 540–430 Maand 350–230 Ma (F.Y.Wu et al., 2011; Y.Liu et al., 2017).Cam-brian–Silurian granitoids are found in the Jiamusi Block and easternErguna and Xing'an blocks (F.Y.Wu et al., 2011).These rocks mayreﬂect subduction, oceanic island accretion and ﬁnal collision of theErguna–Xing'an (Z.Feng et al., 2017) and Jiamusi–Songliao blocks(Wang et al., 2015a, 2015b), as well as westward subduction of anoceanic plate between the Xing'an and Songliao blocks (Y.Liu et al.,2017).Carboniferous-Permian granites are widely distributed in theXing'an and Erguna blocks, expressions of a magmatic ﬂare-up at330–290 Ma (F.Y.Wu et al., 2011; Z.Feng et al., 2015).This ﬂare-upmay record ﬁnal amalgamation of the Xing'an and Songliao blocks (Z.Feng et al., 2015; Y.Liu et al., 2017).In the Junggar segment and Tianshan Orogen of the CAOB in NWChina, granitic magmas intruded during 500–250 Ma (G.J.Tang et al.,2017).

These may have formed by protracted subduction and accretionalong the margins of the Paleo-Asian Ocean between the Siberian andTarim cratons (i.e., Junggar Ocean and South Tianshan Ocean; W.Xiaoet al., 2013; G.J.Tang et al., 2017).Granites around Junggar (Fig. 4A)were mainly emplaced during Carboniferous-Permian time (340–290 Ma) with a few emplaced during the Silurian (430 ± 20 Ma)(G.J.Tang et al., 2017).Carboniferous–Permian plutons consist of A-type and subordinate I-type granites.These intrusions formed theKeramay (or Dulate) arc (Fig. 4A) in the western Junggar (340–290 Ma; J.G.Tang et al., 2010), and two arcs (Dulate-Baytag and Yemaquan; Fig. 4A) in the eastern Junggar (336–307 Ma; C.Zhang et al., 2017)which are interpreted as products of northwestward and northwardsubduction of Junggar oceanic lithosphere, respectively (Prototethys; Y.Xiao et al., 2011).To the south, the granitic complex in the TianshanOrogen is dominated by S-, I-, and A-type granites that were emplacedin two intervals: 470–430 Ma and 310–290 Ma (L.

Long et al., 2011; G.J.Tang et al., 2017).Early Paleozoic igneous rocks are most likelyrelated to protracted northward subduction of the South TianshanOcean and associated accretion events, whereas Late Carboniferous-Permian rocks may be related to the ﬁnal collision between CAOB arcsand continent (Tarim Craton) as the Paleo-Asian Ocean closed (W.Xiaoet al., 2013).The Qilian-Kunlun Orogen (Tier 3) contains abundant Early-MiddlePaleozoic igneous rocks.Magmatic rocks formed at 520–430 Ma in thenorth Qilian orogenic belt are thought to reﬂect northward subductionof the North Qilian oceanic slab (Prototethys) and subsequent Qilian-Alxa (NCC) collision (S.Song et al., 2013).To the south, Ordovician-Silurian granitoids (480–430 Ma) with a peak at ~450 Ma, are dis-tributed throughout the south and central Qilian Orogen (Qilian Block)(H.Yang et al., 2016).The 450 Ma plutons in the Qilian-Kunlun Orogenare composed of I-type, S-type, and adakitic granitoids (Fig. 4A).

Theseintrusions are slightly older than Qaidam UHP rocks in Tier 3(457–420 Ma with a peak at 435–425 Ma; Fig. 3A); together the intru-sions and UHP are thought to mark Qilian-Qaidam collision.To thesouth, Paleozoic igneous rocks are widely distributed around theQaidam Basin.Their ages range from 540 Ma to 340 Ma in the EasternKunlun Orogen, from 560 Ma to 380 Ma in the Altyn Tagh Orogen(Fig. 4A), and from 510 Ma to 360 Ma in the North Qaidam UHP Belt(X.Chen et al., 2015; F.Cheng et al., 2017).These rocks cluster at490–370 Ma and may have formed above two diﬀerent subductionzones.The Kunlun Orogen may record the ﬁnal closure of an arm ofProto-Tethys at 460–430 Ma (F.Cheng et al., 2017).Massive granites in the Cathaysia Block and the Jiangnan Orogenwere emplaced around 460–400 Ma (Y.Wang et al., 2013a; T.Penget al., 2015).They are dominated by peraluminous granites (S-type)containing muscovite, garnet, or tourmaline with minor I-type rocks.

These rocks have evolved εNd(t) (−4.7 to −11.5) and zircon εHf(t)(+2.4 to −19.4), indicating a Proterozoic metapelite and metaigneousrock source with limited juvenile (mantle-derived) components.It re-mains unclear how such voluminous granitic magma was generated.Acollisional model is proposed involving syn-collisional orogen at460–435 Ma, followed by postcollisional extension at 435–400 Ma. 4.2.Mesozoic igneous rocksThe distribution of Mesozoic igneous rocks shifts systematicallyfrom being broadly distributed across China in Triassic time towardconcentration along the eastern and southern margins of China duringthe Cretaceous (Fig. 4B).Triassic igneous rocks are common along thenorth NCC margin, Qinling-Kunlun Orogen, Songpan-Ganzi-Yidun-Qiangtang terranes, Cathaysia Block, and Jiangnan Orogen.Jurassicmagmatic rocks occur in NE China, eastern NCC, and the interior of theSouth China Block.Early Cretaceous magmatic rocks outcrop throughout eastern China including NE China, NCC, and the CathaysiaBlock.

Early Cretaceous igneous rocks are also widespread in the Lhasaterrane of Tibet.Mesozoic igneous rocks of China are often divided into three stages:Indosinian (Triassic), Early Yanshanian (Jurassic), and Late Yanshanian(Cretaceous) (e.g., X.M.Zhou et al., 2006; S.Dong et al., 2008).Allthree stages are sometimes present in a given region, as discussedbelow.Immense volumes of Mesozoic granites and volcanic rocks arewidespread in the Xing'an-Mongolia orogenic belt of the CAOB in NEChina, excluding the area covered by the Songliao Basin (Fig. 4B).Magmatism continued from the Early Triassic to Early Cretaceous(250–100 Ma) and record the tectonic transformation of the regionfrom one dominated by subduction and collision of the E–W trendingPaleo-Asian Ocean (E–W) to one controlled by subduction of the Paciﬁcbeneath N–S oriented convergent margins (F.Y.Wu et al., 2011; W.L.Xu et al., 2013).

The Triassic–Early Jurassic (250–180 Ma) calc-alkalineigneous rocks within the Erguna Block outcrop in a NE–SW belt, re-presenting the magmatic arc related to southward subduction of theMongol–Okhotsk oceanic plate beneath the Erguna Block (J.Tang et al.,2016; Li et al., 2017a, 2017b, 2017c).Triassic magmatism (230–200 Ma) occurred in the central and eastern Xing'an Block, in-cluding A-type granites and bimodal units (230–200 Ma) are identiﬁedin the eastern Songliao Block, likely indicating post-collisional exten-sion related to ﬁnal closure of the Paleo-Asian Ocean between Tier 1 arcterranes and the NCC along the Solonker suture (W.L.Xu et al., 2013).Early-Middle Jurassic calc-alkaline series (200–170 Ma) are widelydistributed and Late Jurassic-Early Cretaceous (165–100 Ma) rocksmainly occur west of the Songliao Basin (F.Y.Wu et al., 2011; W.L.Xuet al., 2013).These rocks are related to either post-orogenic extensionalcollapse of the Mongol–Okhotsk belt or Paleo-Paciﬁc plate subduction(W.L.

Xu et al., 2013; Li et al., 2017a, 2017b, 2017c).Mesozoic igneous rocks in the NCC are divided into ﬁve stages:Early Triassic (254–247 Ma), Middle–Late Triassic (231–221 Ma), EarlyJurassic (190–174 Ma), Middle-Late Jurassic (165–157 Ma), and EarlyR.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9480 Cretaceous (136–115 Ma) (Zhang et al., 2014a, 2014b, 2014c and re-ferences therein).Early Triassic magmatic rocks are mainly highlyfractionated I- and A-type granites that are only found in the northernmargin of the NCC, along the east–west trending Yinshan and Yanshanbelts (Fig. 4A).These reﬂect post-collisional magmatism following theclosure of the Paleo-Asian Ocean between Tier 1 arc terranes and theNCC along the Solonker suture (S.H.Zhang et al., 2010).Middle–LateTriassic igneous rocks primarily occur in the northern and easternmargins of the NCC and Korea, and consist of alkaline granite com-plexes and associated maﬁc-ultramaﬁc rocks related to post-orogenicextension (Zhang et al., 2014a, 2014b, 2014c).

Early Jurassic magmasuites are concentrated in the southern Yanbian region located in thenortheast NCC (Fig. 4A).These plutons include high-K calc-alkaline andshoshonitic series granodiorite-granite, monzodiorite-monzonite-sye-nite; volcanic rocks are basalt-andesite-dacite with minor trachyte.It isunclear whether these represent a continuation of Middle–Late Triassicrelaxation magmatism following collision to form Tier 3 or were causedby subduction of Paciﬁc oceanic lithosphere.Middle-Late Jurassicmagmatism is widely distributed in the central to eastern NCC includingthe Yanshan Belt, Liaodong area, Jiaodong Peninsula, Yanbian–Liaobeiarea, and northern North Korea (Fig. 4B).These plutons are mainly I-type with minor A-type and S-type granites.Most volcanic rocks anddiorites show adakitic signatures.These rocks are calc-alkaline, inter-preted as products of westward subduction of the Paleo-Paciﬁc plate (F.Wu et al., 2008).

Voluminous Early Cretaceous intrusive and volcanicrocks are widespread throughout the eastern and central NCC.A-typegranites with maﬁc microgranular enclaves (MME) and alkaline rocksand basalts are common.Generally, these are considered to be asso-ciated with lithospheric thinning and decratonization (Wu et al., 2005a,2005b), although Paleo-Paciﬁc subduction is also proposed (R.X.Zhuet al., 2012).The Korean Peninsula contains abundant Mesozoic igneous rocks(S.W.Kim et al., 2015; M.G.Zhai et al., 2016).Triassic igneous rocks(253–213 Ma) consist of calc-alkaline and alkaline granites and minorkimberlite.In north-central Korea, Triassic igneous rocks are post-col-lisional plutons related to the CAOB.In south-central Korea, Triassicigneous rocks are either arc magmas related to subduction of Paciﬁcoceanic lithosphere or are post-orogenic plutons related to the Dabie-Sulu Orogen (Tier 3).

Abundant Jurassic rocks (Daebo granitoids)(200–170 Ma) include granodiorite-biotite granite-two mica granite,some with adakitic geochemical signatures.Jurassic igneous rocks re-present the roots of a continental arc associated with subduction of thePaleo-Paciﬁc plate.Cretaceous igneous rocks intruded the northernKorean Peninsula at ~110 Ma and central to southeastern Korea at90–70 Ma.Triassic igneous rocks are widely distributed in the western Qinlingorogenic belt, around the Qaidam Basin, and in the Songpan-Ganzicomplex, Yidun Arc, and Qiangtang Terrane (Fig. 4B).These rocks re-cord the closure of the Paleotethys.In the western Qinling orogenicbelt, magmatism occurred at 252–185 Ma, especially at 225–200 Ma(Wang et al., 2013a, 2013b, 2013d, 2013c; N.Li et al., 2015).The earlystage (250–240 Ma) consisted of I-type granites, adakites and andesites,which may have formed in an arc setting related to subduction of theMianlue Ocean (Paleo-Tethys) between the South Qinling Belt and theYangtze Craton.

Voluminous late stage rocks (225–185 Ma) include I-,S-, and A-type granites, formed during a transition from subduction topost-collisional setting in response to the collision between the NCC andthe Yangtze Craton.Permo-Triassic granitoid plutons were emplacedaround the Qaidam Basin (East-Kunlun, Altyn Tagh, and Qilian oro-genic belts) during 290–210 Ma, clustering at 260–210 Ma (X.Chenet al., 2015; F.Cheng et al., 2017).These rocks are predominantly I-type granites.Slightly younger magmatic rocks were emplaced in theSongpan-Ganzi complex and Yidun Arc (Fig. 4B) at 220–195 Ma and230–210 Ma, respectively (Zhang et al., 2014a, 2014b, 2014c; T.Wuet al., 2017).These rocks are mostly I-type granites with some adakitesin the Songpan-Ganzi complex and S-type granites in the Yidun Arc.Coeval magmatic rocks are widespread in the Qiangtang Terrane ofTibet.Triassic (236–219 Ma) adakite–magnesian–andesite and Nb-en-riched basaltic rocks are found along the northern margin of theNorthern Qiangtang subterrane (Q.Wang et al., 2008).

Triassic I-type(230–210 Ma in the west; 250–220 Ma in the east) and S-type granites(228–223 Ma) occur in the central Qiangtang belt along the LongmuTso-Shuanghu suture zone (Fig. 3A) (L.Lu et al., 2017).Although tec-tonic scenarios for closing the Paleo-Tethys remain hotly debated,subduction of the Tethyan Ocean slab (i.e., Longmu Tso-ShuanghuOcean and Jinshajiang Ocean) and multiple collision events betweenvarious terranes in the Late Triassic produced Triassic igneous rocksaround Qaidam, and in the Songpan-Ganzi complex, Yidun Arc, andQiangtang Terrane (Pullen et al., 2008; J.W.Zi et al., 2013).Two E–W trending magmatic belts developed in the Qiangtang-Lhasa terranes during the Triassic-Late Miocene (D.C.Zhu et al., 2011,Zhu et al., 2017b, 2017a).The north belt was active from the MiddleJurassic to Early Cretaceous (170–105 Ma) with a magmatic ﬂare-up at130–110 Ma along the central and northern Lhasa subterrane andsouthern Qiangtang subterrane.

A Jurassic Bangong-Amdo arc (170–150 Ma) and Early Cretaceous arc-like rocks (125–105 M) alongthe southern Qiangtang subterrane are related to northward subductionof the Bangong-Nujiang Tethyan slab and subsequent Qiangtang-Lhasacollision (D.C.Zhu et al., 2016).Associated slab-derived adakites andasthenosphere-derived OIB-type (oceanic island basalt) rocks at ca. 156 Ma suggests mid-ocean ridge subduction at this time (S.M.Li et al.,2016b).Magmatic rocks in the northern and central Lhasa subterranes,however, may be either products of southward subduction of the Ban-gong-Nujiang Tethys (Fig. 3A) or northward subduction of the Neo-tethys (D.C.Zhu et al., 2011, 2013).The southern belt of igneous rocks in Tibet began to develop in theLate Triassic (~220 Ma) and lasted until the Late Miocene (~10 Ma)along the southern Lhasa subterrane (W.Q.Ji et al., 2009; D.C.Zhuet al., 2017a).Triassic-Jurassic (220–140 Ma) igneous rocks remain lessunderstood because of a lack of geochronologic and geochemical data.

Cretaceous–Neogene rocks, including the Gangdese batholith and Lin-zizong volcanic successions, formed at 120–10 Ma with peaks at95–90 Ma and 52–48 Ma and a magmatic lull at 40–20 Ma (D.C.Zhuet al., 2017a).The 120–40 Ma calc-alkaline magmatic rocks are inter-preted as products of northward subduction of the Neotethys with ridgesubduction at 95–90 Ma, and following India-Asia collision at ~55 Ma(D.C.Zhu et al., 2016).The younger 20–10 Ma igneous rocks (in-cluding ultrapotassic and adakitic rocks) reﬂect postcollisional mag-matism in response to continued India-Asia convergence and Tibetancrustal thickening (S.L.Chung et al., 2005).Abundant Mesozoic igneous rocks are exposed in the CathaysiaBlock and the Jiangnan Orogen in South China (Fig. 4B) (X.M.Zhouet al., 2006; Y.Wang et al., 2013a; L.Shu et al., 2015).These arepredominantly granites and associated minor basaltic to andesitic lavas.

Indosinian (Triassic) intrusions consist of abundant (~60%) stronglyperaluminous S-type granites (two-mica monzonitic, biotite monzoniticand muscovite granite), which are characterized by the presence ofgarnet or cordierite and negative εNd(t) (−6.4 to −10.8) and zirconεHf(t) (−2.0 to −20.2), indicating crustal sources with insigniﬁcantaddition of juvenile mantle-derived magma (Y.Wang et al., 2007).Thetectonic regime for these rocks has been long debated.Several modelshave been proposed, including a ﬂat-slab westward subduction of thePaciﬁc plate (Li and Li, 2007), Alpine-type collision within the SouthChina Block (Hsü et al., 1990), and intracontinental convergence bywhich the South China Block was squeezed between the NCC and theIndochina block (Y.Wang et al., 2007; L.Shu et al., 2015).

EarlyYanshanian (Jurassic) granitoids dated at 180–150 Ma with an age peakat 158 Ma are distributed in the interior of the South China Blockwhereas Late Yanshanian (Cretaceous) granitoids and volcanic rocksshowing two age peaks (130–120 Ma and 107–87 Ma) are distributedalong a NE-trending magmatic zone through the coastal provinces(Fig. 4B).I-, S- and A-type granites are common and these magmasR.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9481 originated from the partial melting of the Proterozoic basement withvarying contributions from mantle-derived magmas.This Cretaceousigneous activity reﬂected intraplate extension/rifting (sometimes re-ferred to as the Southeastern China Basin and Range Province; Gilderet al., 1991) accompanying northwesterly oblique subduction of theIzanagi and/or Paleo-Paciﬁc plate beneath SE Eurasia (X.M.Zhou et al.,2006).Delamination of the lower crust and mantle lithosphere mayhave accompanied subduction and extension (Wang et al., 2015a,2015b; Wang et al., 2016a, 2016b).

Some Chinese authors persist inusing the name “Paleo-Paciﬁc plate” instead of more widely accepted“Izanagi plate” and in assuming that the Paleo-Paciﬁc (Izanagi)-Paciﬁcspreading ridge was subducted during the Cretaceous and was re-sponsible for generating Cretaceous adakitic magmas, but this ignoresglobal plate reconstructions showing Izanagi-Paciﬁc ridge subductionoccurred during the Eocene (Seton et al., 2012). 4.3.Large igneous provincesKröner et al.(2014) noted that much of the juvenile crustal growthin Central Asia occurred during the Late Permian and Early Mesozoic,after closure of the Paleo-Asian ocean (Proto-Tethys) and collision toform Tier 1 (CAOB), and inferred that this may have been related tomajor plume activity.There are three large igneous provinces (LIPs) inChina that may be related to these plumes, two of Permian age and oneof Cretaceous age.The Emeishan LIP in Tier 4 is China's most famous LIP (Fig. 4A).

Itcovers ~3 × 105 km2 and consists mostly of ﬂood basalts with somemaﬁc-ultramaﬁc (possible komatiites) and minor rhyolitic rocks (Shellnutt, 2014).These rocks were emplaced during a brief (< 1 mil-lion year) episode (H.Huang et al., 2016), contemporaneous with thelate Capitanian (~260 Ma) mass extinction.The Emeishan LIP hostsnumerous world-class base metal deposits.The Early Permian Tarim LIP occurs in the Tarim basin in NW Chinaand is buried by the Taklamagan desert (Fig. 4A).It may extend over anarea of 2.5–3.0 × 105 km2.Three episodes of magmatism have beenidentiﬁed (Y.G.Xu et al., 2014).The ﬁrst episode (~300 Ma) isrepresented by small-volume diamondiferous kimberlites; the secondepisode (~290 Ma) is characterized by bimodal magmatism, includingﬂood basalts and low-Nb–Ta rhyolites; and the third episode (~280 Ma)is also dominated by bimodal magmatism, consisting of ultra- maﬁc–maﬁc–felsic intrusions and dyke swarms, syenites and A-typegranites.

The ~290 Ma ﬂood basalts are spread all over the region,whereas ~300 Ma kimberlites and ~280 Ma bimodal rocks only occurin the Bachu Uplift (Fig. 4A).Igneous rocks of the Early Cretaceous Comei LIP cover ~0.4 × 105 km2 in the Tethyan Himalaya in southeastern Tibet(Fig. 4A).These rocks and were emplaced at ~132 Ma and so aresomehow related to Late Yanshannian igneous activity.They includeabundant volcanic rocks (mostly basalt with some felsic lavas), volu-minous maﬁc intrusions and minor ultramaﬁc rocks (D.C.Zhu et al.,2009; Y.Xia et al., 2014).The Comei igneous rocks and Bunbury Basalts(~132 Ma) in SW Australia have been linked into a single Comei-Bun-bury LIP on the basis of their similarity in emplacement age and tec-tonic reconstruction of eastern Gondwana.This LIP was likely causedby the Kerguelen mantle plume (D.C.Zhu et al., 2009). 4.4.Cenozoic basalts Cenozoic volcanic rocks are widely distributed in eastern China andare part of the circum-Paciﬁc volcanic belt, extending over 4000 kmfrom north to south (H.

Zou et al., 2000).They are associated withmajor regional faults, like the Tan-Lu fault.These volcanic rocks arepredominantly alkali basalts, but tholeiitic basalts also occur (Wanget al., 2011a, 2011b).These basalts share similar OIB-like character-istics of trace elements and moderately depleted and enriched radio-genic isotope compositions.They may originate from partial melting inupwelling asthenosphere and/or subcontinental lithospheric mantleaccompanying backarc extension above the subducting Paciﬁc plate(Wang et al., 2011a, 2011b), although a deep mantle plume may alsocontribute signiﬁcantly to this magmatism (J.-I.Kimura et al., 2018). 0 500 1000 km 70°E 80°E 90°E 100°E 110°E 120°E 130°E 140°E 40°N 30°N 40°N 30°N 20°N 100°E 90°E Mongolia Kazakhstan India Craton 0 500 1000 km Contractional Basins Composite Basins Extensional Basins Mesozoic-Cenozoic Sedimentary BasinsTarim B.Ordos B.Songliao B.Erlian B.Hailar B.Jungar B.Tuha B.Qiangtang B.Zhujiangkou B.East China Sea B.Okinawa Trough B.Bohai Bay B.

Busab B. 110°E 120°E Sichuan B.Jianghan B.Simao B.Subei-S.Yellow Sea B.Cuoqen B.S North China B.Gamba Tingri B.Yingen B.Biru B.Qiadam B.Fig. 5.Sedimentary Basins of China.Note the diﬀerent styles of basins in western China vs. eastern China.Modiﬁed after He and Zheng, 2016.R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9482 0 500 1000 km 70°E 80°E 90°E 100°E 110°E 40°N 30°N 40°N 30°N 20°N 100°E 90°E 0 500 1000 km 100°E 90°E 110°E 120°E Drainage basins Amur River Tarim River Yellow River Yangtze River Yarlung River Pearl River Rivers Suture Zones Yarlung (Brahmaputra) RiverYangtze River Nujiang River Jinsha River Mingjiang River Huai River Pearl River Songhua River Tarim River Yellow River River 0 400 2000 1600 2400 1200 3200 2800 800 3600 (A) (B) Amur n = 101 n = 199 n = 258 n = 108 n = 93 (C) Tarim (D) Yellow (E) Yangtze (F) Pearl 12 8 4 30 20 10 15 9 3 8 6 4 2 8 6 4 2 180 290 450 445 200 780 1900 1800 2400 340 165 430 860 Tier 2 Tier 1 Tier 3 Tier 4 Tier 5 Fig. 6.

Detrital zircon U-Pb zircon ages for sediments from ﬁve great rivers ﬂowing across China: A) Major rivers of China and their drainage basins.B) Age histogram for Amur Riverdetrital zircons (Safonova et al., 2010); C) Age histogram for Tarim River detrital zircons (Rittner et al., 2016); D) Age histogram for Yellow River detrital zircons (J.Yang et al., 2011); E)Age histogram for Yangtze River detrital zircons (He et al., 2013a, 2013b); F) Age histogram for Pearl River detrital zircons (Xu et al., 2016a, 2016b).R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9483 5.Sedimentary basins Sedimentary basins are important components of China's crust andpreserve a high-ﬁdelity record of climate and relief development be-ginning in the Precambrian through Phanerozoic time.There are 424basins in China > 1 km2 in area, however only 10 sedimentary basinscover > 10,000 km2 (He and Zheng, 2016).Six of these great basins areonshore; from NW to SE these are the Junggar, Tarim, Qaidam, Ordos,Songliao, and Sichuan basins (Fig. 5).

The Tarim, Ordos, and Sichuanbasins share Early Paleozoic marine sequences.The Junggar, Qaidam,and Songliao basin ﬁlls only date back to the Late Paleozoic.There arealso four important oﬀshore basins: Bohai Bay, Subei - South Yellow SeaBasin, East China Basin, and Zhujiangkou (sometimes called the PearlRiver Mouth) Basin (Fig. 5).The oﬀshore basins and the Songliao Basinhave similar backarc settings to Cenozoic extensional basins that fringeeastern Eurasia, including the Miocene Sea of Japan and a still-activerift, the Okinawa Trough.Onshore basins in western China are much older and formed dif-ferently than the extensional Songliao and oﬀshore basins (Fig. 5).TheJunggar Basin is built on Tier 1 crust and contains at least 16 km ofsediment, mostly Late Paleozoic through Mesozoic but also signiﬁcant(4 km) Neogene ﬁll.The Tarim Basin is by far the largest, covering 560,000 km2, andlargely burying the Tarim Craton of Archean to Paleoproterozoic age,the western part of Tier 2.

The Tarim Basin is covered by a thick (up to17 km) sequence of Mesozoic to Cenozoic continental sediments un-derlain by Paleozoic shallow marine sedimentary rocks (Y.Wang et al.,2013b), including up to 5500 m of Neogene sediments (Jiang et al., inpress).Six major depositional episodes are recorded in the ﬁll of thisbasin: late Early Paleozoic, Middle Paleozoic, Late Paleozoic, EarlyMesozoic, Late Mesozoic, and Neogene (He and Zheng, 2016).The left-lateral Altyn Tagh fault separates the Tarim Basin to thenorth from the Qaidam Basin to the south.The Qaidam Basin covers120,000 km2 and contains about 8 km thickness of Mesozoic andCenozoic sediments, two-thirds of which was deposited in Neogenetime (J.Zhao et al., 2013).The Ordos Basin is the second largest interior basin in China and isbuilt on the western NCC (Tier 2).Ordos Basin sedimentation evolvedfrom an Early Paleozoic marine shelf to a continental basin beginningduring the Carboniferous.

Thick Triassic sediments in the Ordos Basinmanifest strong tectonic activity in the Central China Orogen to thesouth (Tier 3).The Ordos Basin contains up to 5 km thickness of sedi-ment.The basin is underlain by a high P-wave velocity in the uppermantle (8.4 km/s), indicating strong, thick lithospheric mantle (Tianet al., 2014a, 2014b).The Songliao Basin occupies 260,000 km2, is built on CAOB accretedterranes in the north and contains up to 6 km of sedimentary rocks.LatePaleozoic marine sedimentation was succeeded by a Late Permian ter-restrial basin.Backarc extension beginning in Jurassic time and con-tinuing through the Cretaceous ﬁlled the basin.The Sichuan Basin encompasses 190,000 km2 on the western ﬂankof the South China Block.The basement of the Sichuan Basin is Archeanto Paleoproterozoic in age and sediments are up to 8 km thick, con-sisting mostly of Triassic and Jurassic sediments.The Sichuan Basin wasonce a marine shelf from Ediacaran through the Triassic and has been acontinental basin since Triassic time.

Mesozoic and younger basins are common on China's easternmargin, both onshore (Songliao, western Bohai Bay and Subei basins)and oﬀshore (eastern Bohai Basin, Yellow Sea Basin, East China SeaBasin, and Zhujiangkou Basin (Fig. 5).The ﬁll of these basins beganwith Early Paleozoic marine sediments to Permian coastal sediments.For most of these basins, extension began in Late Jurassic and EarlyCretaceous and continued into Paleogene time.About half of the sedi-ment ﬁll is Cenozoic in age.Eastern China basins such as Songliao and Bohai Bay are mostlyextensional, but those of the western interior such as Tarim, Junggar,Ordos, Sichuan, and Qaidam basins are not.Carroll et al.(2010) com-mented on the unique nature of western China basins, coining the term“walled basins” for these long-lived (up to ~250 m.y.) continental ba-sins surrounded by mountains.Carroll et al.(2010) noted that thesebasins are broadly contractional but diﬀer from classic foreland basinsby having loads and shortening on all sides.

They typically recordmultiple episodes of terrestrial sedimentation reﬂecting episodic upliftof the surrounding highlands.The mechanisms for maintaining stableinteriors for millions of years is unclear, but likely involves a strongerlithosphere beneath the basin than beneath the surrounding regions.These basins are built on ancient crust, further indicating that litho-spheric strength is important for basin evolution.Such regions of stronglithosphere resist deformation at the same time focusing deformationaround its margins.Western China walled basins are largely post-col-lisional – that is, they mostly evolved after Late Paleozoic collisionformed the CAOB and Triassic collision formed the Dabie Shan-Qinling-Qilian-Kunlun orogen.However, elements of some of these basins wereestablished before these collisions.Late Permian and younger ﬁll ofwestern China sedimentary basins is almost exclusively terrigineous.

Extensional basins such as the Songliao and Bohai and oﬀshorebasins reﬂect backarc basin extension accompanying rollback of sub-ducting oceanic lithosphere of the Paciﬁc Ocean, with additional for-cing in Cenozoic time due to escape tectonics accompanying collision ofEurasia with India.Extensional basins developed in East China duringthe Late Jurassic and Early Cretaceous were accompanied by the for-mation of metamorphic core complexes.In summary, West China basins mark isolated regions of lithosphericstrength and East China basins mark elongate regions of backarc li-thospheric weakness. 6.Detrital zircons of river sedimentsComparing U-Pb ages of detrital zircons of modern sediments for themajor rivers of China is an independent way to gain insights into theage of China's crust.These great rivers carry sediments eroded fromigneous and metamorphic rocks of the crust as well as eroded and re-deposited from overlying sediments.

Because the 5 great rivers of Chinaﬂow from the west to east, the sediments (and the zircons) theytransport downstream, preferentially sample the E–W crustal tiers.From north to south the rivers of interest are: the Amur, the Tarim, theYellow, the Yangtze, and the Pearl (Fig. 6A).The Amur River (Heilong Jiang) is ~4440 km long and is the 10thlongest river in the world.The Amur River drainage basin, locatedbetween China and Russia, encompasses 2,824,000 km2 (Safonovaet al., 2010).The portion of the basin located in China lies in Tier 1.Asample of river sediment, taken 350 miles upstream from the river'smouth, shows a main U-Pb age cluster between 500 and 50 Ma(Fig. 6B), as expected for sediment eroded from the CAOB (Tier 1) alongwith Mesozoic igneous rocks associated with Paciﬁc plate subductionplus subordinate contributions from older rocks including the SiberianCraton (Safonova et al., 2010).

The age histogram (Fig. 6B) shows thelargest peak at 100–200 Ma with the next largest peak at 450–250 Ma,the third largest peak at 2.8–2.3 Ga, and the fourth largest peak at~1.8 Ga.The Tarim River is about 2000 km long and drains ~557,000 km2 inwestern Tier 1 before terminating in saline lakes in the Tarim Basin.U-Pb zircon ages for sediments sampled at three places along the river(sites 04, 07, and 26 studied by Rittner et al., 2016) are shown inFig. 6C.U-Pb ages for zircons from these sediments yield strong peaksof Early and Late Paleozoic age, with less abundant Neoproterozoic, andscattered ages back to the Archean.The abundance of Paleozoic agesand paucity of Paleoproterozoic and Archean ages are surprising inlight of what we understand about the rocks surrounding the Tarimbasin.The Yellow River (Huang He) is ~5700 km long from its headwatersin northern Tibet to the Bohai Sea.It is the second-longest river inR.J.Stern et al.

Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9484 China and the 6th longest river in the world; its drainage basin en-compasses ~750,000 km2.Much of its middle course ﬂows across theKunlun-Qinling Orogen (Tier 3), although most of its lower course ﬂowsacross Tier 2 (NCC).Zircons from three samples of river sand from themouth of the river, ~500 km upstream, and ~800 km upstream, weredated by J.Yang et al.(2009) (Fig. 6A).These samples show similar agespectra, with four major age peaks (Fig. 6D).The largest age peak isPermo-Triassic (200–300 Ma), the second largest is Cambro-Ordovician(400–500 Ma), the third major age peak is Paleoproterozoic (~1.9 Ga),and the fourth largest peak is Early Paleoproterozoic (2.5–2.1 Ga).Theﬁrst two peaks are likely derived from Tier 2 exposures in Kunlun-Qinling.The abundance of Precambrian zircons is consistent with anorigin from erosion of the NCC.

It is somewhat surprising that Archeanages are rare, although Paleoproterozoic ages (1.6–2.5 Ga) are common, suggesting that the abundance of Archean rocks in the NCChas been overestimated.Earliest Paleoproterozoic (2.5–2.4 Ga) ages arewidespread in the NCC, with voluminous granitoids and felsic volcanicrocks in this age range.It is also not surprising that there is a signiﬁcantdecrease in Yellow River zircon ages after ~1.8 Ga, because this is whenthe NCC stabilized.The largest peaks are for 200–500 Ma zircons,probably derived from the Kunlun-Qinling orogen.There are two sub-peaks in this broad Phanerozoic peak; Early Paleozoic and Triassic(Fig. 6D).The Yangtze River (Changjiang) is ~6300 km long from itsFig. 7.Magnetic and gravity anomaly maps of China.A) Bouguer anomaly gravity map of China.Downloaded from http://www.gifex.com/fullsize-en/2011-08-03-14241/Bouguer-gravity-anomaly-in-China.html.B) Magnetic anomaly map of China.

The magnetic anomaly values were obtained from the global magnetic grid EMAG2: Earth Magnetic Anomaly Grid(2-arc-minute resolution). http://geomag.org/models/emag2.html.R.J.Stern et al.Earth-Science Reviews 179 (2018) 72–9485 headwaters in Tibet to its mouth in the East China Sea.It is the longestriver in Asia and the third longest in the world after the Nile and theAmazon.The drainage basin of the Yangtze encompasses ~1.8 mil-lion km2 and mostly ﬂows across Tier 4 crust, including the Qiangtangterrane of Tibet, Songpan-Ganzi terrane, and the South China Block.Heet al.(2013a, 2013b) analyzed 108 detrital zircons from ChangxingIsland in the mouth of the Yangtze River.The age spectrum (Fig. 6E)shows major peaks of ~1.8 Ga, ~900–800 Ma, and ~300–150 Ma,which are consistent with the characteristic basement ages of Tier 3 andthe South China Block.The Pearl River (Zhujiang) is ~2400 km long and is China's thirdlongest river.Its ~410,000 km2 drainage basin is entirely in the SouthChina Block of Tier 4.Xu et al.

(2016a, 2016b) studied detrital zirconsfrom the Pearl River in southernmost China (Fig. 6F).The largest agepeak is Triassic-Jurassic, the second largest peak is Tonian (900–800 Ma), and the third largest peak is Ordovician (~400 Ma).Scattered Paleoproterozoic and Archean ages are also reported but arevery subordinate.This is consistent with what we know about the crustof this region. 7.Geophysics 7.1.Gravity and magneticsThe Bouguer gravity map of China (Fig. 7A) approximates an in-verse crustal thickness map, with gravity lows in the west markingwhere crust is thick (e.g., in Tibet) and gravity highs in the east wherethe crust is thin.The Bouguer gravity map thus does not reﬂect the ﬁvetiers discussed above because it shows crustal thickness, not age.Thestrong gradients in crustal thickness and thus gravity are E–W, not N–Sas seen in the ﬁve tiers.In fact, China can be divided at about 110° Elongitude into a western region of thick (75–40 km thick) and aneastern region of thin (40–30 km thick) continental crust.

The thickestcrust in the world underlies Tibet but crust of the Tianshan north of theTarim Basin is also unusually thick, almost 55 km.Thick crust in wes-tern China is responsible for the most elevated lands on Earth.Thincrust in eastern China is associated with lowlands dominated byﬂoodplains and deltas.This gradient in crustal thickness exerted theﬁrst-order control on the eastward course of the major rivers.The magnetic anomaly map (Fig. 7B) reveals the ﬁve tiers of Chinabecause these show diﬀerent magnetic textures and trends.Tier 1, theCAOB, is highly magnetized, deﬁning a high frequency belt in north-ernmost China.Tier 2, the Archean-Paleoproterozoic nuclei of NCC andTarim, is characterized by broad magnetic anomalies.Tier 3, the Sulu-Dabie-Qinling-Qilian-Kunlun collision zone, is marked by moderate-wavelength magnetic anomalies parallel to the WNW-ENE trend of theorogen.Tier 4, South China Block and Tibet, are marked by broadanomalies, with NE-SW trend along the Paciﬁc margins.

A broad N–Smagnetic high marks the region between Tibet and South China.TheNE-SW trend of the China passive margin dominates the magneticstructure of Tier 5 (Taiwan). 7.2.Active source seismic imagingDeep seismic imaging also provides important insights into China'scrustal structure.Over a decade ago, S.Li et al.(2006) noted that about90 seismic refraction/wide angle reﬂection lines with a cumulativelength of 60,000 km had been completed in mainland China.Since thattime, many more active source seismic experiments have been carriedout.Table 2 lists and Fig. 8 shows the approximate locations of 12 re-presentative proﬁles, all of which have been published since 2003.Mostof these proﬁles were wide-angle reﬂection/refraction studies usingexplosions as seismic sources.Fig. 9 shows the results of each of these12 seismic proﬁles, from NW to SE.Each is brieﬂy described below.The Central Asian Orogenic Belt (CAOB) in NW China was studiedby Y.Wang et al.(2003).

They conducted a 1100 km long seismic re-fraction study (Fig. 8, line A) and found that the crust was about 50 kmthick (Fig. 9A).The thickest crust (56 km) lies beneath the AltaiMountains and the thinnest (46 km) lies beneath the Junggar Basin.Thecrust has 3 layers, with Vp's of 6.0–6.3 km/s, 6.3–6.6 km/s, and6.9–7.0 km/s.The entire proﬁle is underlain by a 15–30 km thick high-velocity (6.9–7.0 km/s) lower crustal layer interpreted by Y.Wang et al.(2003) as maﬁc granulite.They also interpreted the consistent 3-layerstratiﬁcation to indicate that the crust experienced partial melting anddiﬀerentiation associated with terrane accretion.To the east, the CAOB and its accretion to the margin of the NorthChina Craton in NE China was studied by W.Li et al.(2013).Theycarried out a 450 km long wide angle reﬂection/refraction line (Fig. 8,line B) and found that the crust thinned from the SE (47 km) to NW(42 km; Fig. 9B).

They concluded that thicker crust beneath the NCCresulted from Jurassic compression and that the ﬂat and shallower(40 km deep) Moho beneath the CAOB reﬂected extension.They iden-tiﬁed 4 crustal layers.Thickening of the uppermost crust correspondedto a broad region of outcropping granitic rocks, suggesting the presenceof a subduction- or collision- related batholith.NW dipping reﬂectors,perhaps Paleozoic thrust faults related to CAOB accretion, were iden-tiﬁed beneath the CAOB and northernmost NCC and these reﬂectorsappear to be cut by younger intrusions.Lower crust Vp values are re-latively low (6.58–6.75 km/s), indicating that it is not very maﬁc.Vpvalues show signiﬁcant lateral velocity variations between the NCC andCAOB suggesting that the CAOB and NCC suture lies near the north-ernmost limit of outcropping Paleoproterozoic and Archean rocks.The CAOB and western Sangilao Basin in far NE China was probedby H.Hou et al.(2015).

This crust was mostly created during the Pa-leozoic but also experienced complex Mesozoic tectonism due to sub-duction of the Mongol–Okhotsk Ocean (MOO) plate and associatedcollision.MOO closure was followed by subduction of the Paciﬁcoceanic lithosphere to the east.H.Hou et al.(2015) carried out a400 km ~E–W long reﬂection proﬁle (Fig. 8, line C) that revealed manycomplex reﬂectors in the middle and upper crust, interpreted asTable 2 Representative seismic proﬁles of China's crustal structure.Map location Region [参考文献及致谢部分略——按要求不收录]

中国大地构造单元划分

潘桂棠, 肖庆辉, 陆松年, 等. 2009. 中国地质, 36(1)（扫描版 RapidOCR 识别，可能有识别误差） · 原文：http://geochina.cgs.gov.cn/cn/article/id/20090101

提要：以大地构造相为原则划分中国 9 个一级构造单元（陆块区与造山系）+ 56 个二级构造单元；【含山东】华北陆块区、苏鲁造山系（胶南-威海造山带）的归属

第36卷第1期 中国地质 Vol.36,No.1 2009年2月 GEOLOGY IN CHINA Feb.,2009 中国大地构造单元划分 潘桂棠1肖庆辉2陆松年3邓晋福4冯益民5张克信6 张智勇2王方国1邢光福7郝国杰3冯艳芳2 (1. 成都地质矿产研究所,四川成都610082;2.中国地质调查局发展研究中心,北京100037;3.天津地质矿产研究所,天津300170;4.中国地质大学(北京),北京100083;5.西安地质矿产研究所,陕西西安710054;6. 中国地质大学地质过程与矿产资源国家重点实验室,湖北武汉430074;7.南京地质矿产研究所,江苏南京210016)提要:中国大地构造形成演化与大地构造分区研究已有百余年的历史,整体论述中国构造分区,都以不同学派对中国大陆地壳形成演化的不同认识论和方法论,有不同的方案。以黄汲清先生等多旋回构造观、王鸿祯先生等历史大地构热欣热用梁系科热转准热热昂热梁热插液花和 成,在全国起指导作用,影响既广泛且深远。板块构造单元划分是当前板块构造细结构研究的关键问题。它既是板块构造研究的理论问题,也是区域地质研究和成矿预测评价亟待解决的实际问题。本文的大地构造分区图的编制是以地层划分和对比、沉积建造、火山岩建造、侵入岩浆活动、变质变形等地质记录为基础,承接融合中国“三大主流大地构造观”的经典划分理念,在板块构造—地球动力学理论指导下,以成矿规律和矿产能源预测的需求为基点,以不同规模相对稳定的古老陆块区和不同时期的造山系大地构造相环境时空结构分析为主线,以特定区域主构造事件形成的优势大地构造相的时空结构组成和存在状态为划分构造单元的基本原则,划分出中国的大地构造环境主要由陆块区和造山系组成为9个一级构造单元,以及相应的56个二级构造单元。中国大地构造研究还存在一系列重大科学问题,较准确地划分尚需很长时间的不懈努力。 关键词:大地构造;构造单元;陆块区;造山系;多岛弧盆系中图分类号:P544 文献标志码:A 文章编号:1000-3657(2009)01-0001-28大地构造分区又叫大地构造单元划分,是大地 需结合特定区域的地质特征进一步厘定。本文的目 构造研究成果的表达形式之一,可直接服务于资源 的是在中国各区域大地构造单元划分的基础上加以 预测需求,作为成矿地质背景或油气盆地分析以及 集成综合,用现代大陆地壳演化观点思考,强调“将 地质灾害评估的基点。

除叠加的构造-岩 带、弧盆系、地块;陆块区的二级分区分不同时代、不 浆岩带外,盖层主要按地层形成的构造背景及大地 同构造环境的古陆块。 构造优势相划分不同的构造单元(盆地类型)。 4 中国一级大地构造单元的基本特征 (1)依据陆块区不同演化阶段不同基底和盖层 的岩石建造组合,可划分为陆块(含陆核)作为二级 1)华北陆块区 单元。 华北陆块区,由5个规模较小的陆块及其之间 (2)华北陆块新太古代—古元古代的地质记录 的洋壳(?)消减、碰撞汇聚(1900~1800Ma)而成统一以及扬子、塔里木陆块中元古代—新元古代地质记 的华北古大陆,记录了中国最古老地壳的形成过程, 录,保存该时期基底陆壳物质的组成、物质来源和形 经历了古、中太古代陆核的孕育,新太古代初始克拉 成环境,特别是由侵入岩构成的岩浆弧为标志: 通化,古元古代结晶基底形成,中元古代扩张裂陷, TTG和DMG组合;以及表壳岩的火山-沉积记录、 中、新元古代一古生代稳定盖层沉积和中生代陆内 岩石组合、地球化学、热事件等特征,可将基底划分 构造作用等演化阶段[38-40]。由前新太古代地质体构出岩浆弧、裂谷等三级构造单元。 成陆核,如陈台沟—沂水陆核、迁西陆核、兴和陆核, (3)大尺度范围盖层细结构的划分,依关键地质 其表壳岩系和变质深成岩系均经历了麻粒岩构成的 事件形成的大地构造相及沉积盆地的性质、类型、序 角闪岩相变质作用,表现为一系列地壳垂向增生占 列、时代和空间分布特征,如:被动陆缘盆地、陆表海 主导的穹隆构造[41,42]。 盆地、碳酸岩台地、陆缘裂谷、陆内裂谷、断陷盆地、 以五台一太行岩浆弧(新太古代)为中轴带,其 压陷盆地等作为三级构造单元。 东西原陆块具有不同的物质组成、结构和演化特征。 其东部迁西群和鞍山群有38.4亿年的同位素年龄 3 中国大地构造分区方案 数据。西部迄今有大于3000Ma的地壳年龄数据报 中国大地构造分区是在对中国大地构造研究的 导,主体以含炭高铝质变质的孔兹岩系组合为主。东 基础上,结合特定构造部位和构造时期所发生的主 部为新太古代花岗岩-绿岩组合[43]。五台—太行中轴要地质事件,并将在这一事件中所形成特定的岩 带发育高压麻粒岩带,一些学者认为该带即为东西 石-构造组合厘定为优势大地构造相(亚相),分析 古陆块的碰撞结合带[44-47]。古元古代的辽河—青龙其与相邻构造部位优势大地构造相之间的时空联系 河陆缘裂谷记录了原陆块复杂的演化过程。

VII-9 崇左弧盆系 VII-9-2崇左岛弧(P-T₂) VII-9-3凭祥蛇绿混杂岩带(P-T₂) VIII 印度陆块 VIII-1-1 锡伐利克后碰撞压陷盆地 IX 菲律宾造山系 IX-1 台东弧盆系 IX-1-1奇美火山弧(N2-Q1)(菲律宾板块) 8 中 国 地 质 2009年 发育一套原岩为河流-滨浅海沉积和中基性或酸性 件,而是伸展断陷作用的一种表现形式[59-61],而且晚火山岩[48,49]。

侏罗世后城组火山岩具有双峰式组合特征[62]。 汇聚碰撞后1800~1600 Ma期间,长城纪裂解早白垩世(145.5~99.6 Ma)时期,华北陆块发育事件群表现为基性岩墙群、超钾质火山岩、碱性花岗 变质核杂岩、底侵热隆构造、大面积火山喷发碱性花 岩等一系列热-构造事件的岩石组合[50]。在华北陆块岗岩、基性岩墙群,地热流值和地温梯度增高、大规 北缘有中元古代的燕山裂谷、渣尔泰—白云鄂博裂 模成矿作用、郯庐断裂左行走滑拉分盆地的形成等。 谷,华北陆块南缘的熊耳—西阳河裂谷[51,52]。 表明华北陆块区进入全面的伸展阶段,这已为中国 自1600Ma以后,中元古代蓟县系仍发育了具 地质界所共识。大多数学者将这一时期岩石圈减薄、 同生滑塌体系的碳酸盐台地、火山活动及黑色页岩 岩石圈根拆沉、或者软流圈上涌的大变革时期[59,63-66]。 缺氧事件等,相对稳定的伸展构造状态一直持续到 2)扬子陆块区 1200Ma。其中,燕山裂谷最近被认为是弧后扩张的前新元古代的扬子陆块区克拉通基底太古宙及 产物[53]。新元古代青白口系内虽然存在几个不整合古元古代地层出露很少,依据地质、地球物理资料,通 面,但是可能为哥伦比亚超大陆解体后,华北古陆被 常推断四川盆地之下为陆核。以湖北崆岭岩群保存范 动大陆边缘上的伸展不整合或层序界面(上超或下超) 围较大[67,68]。从东冲河杂岩的花岗片麻岩中获得大于不整合,均不属于造山不整合。新元古代南华纪冰川 3000Ma的数据[69],黄陵背斜水月寺岩群的辉石片麻地质事件具全球规模,有“雪球地球”之说,但华北陆 岩(原岩奥长花岗岩)的锆石U-Pb等时线上交点年 块似乎未见冰期记录,在华北南缘和西南缘可见震旦 龄(2891±160) Ma,侵入其中的花岗闪长岩锆石 Pb-系罗圈组和正目观组陆相冰碛岩,比扬子陆块南沱冰 Pb 等时线年龄2315 Ma[70],而以中元古代中低级变质碛层年轻,而与全吉山地块红铁沟组相当[43,54,55]。表明火山-沉积岩系广泛发育为特色的上扬子古陆西缘 华北大陆在元古宙时与扬子相距很远,一直持续至 的碧口群、通木梁群、白水群、黄水河群,康滇带的会 600 Ma左右。 理群、昆阳群,东南缘梵净山群、四堡群、冷家溪群,以寒武—奥陶纪是华北陆块区又一次碳酸岩台地 及中—新元古代赣北的九岭群、双桥山群、皖南的上 形成期,以碳酸盐岩为主夹少量细碎屑岩的浅海陆 溪群、浙西北的双溪坞群为代表[71-76。

总体表现为扬架沉积广布,构成了陆块区的盖层。但在贺兰山及鄂 子周边由岛弧-弧后盆地组成的弧-盆系火山-浊流 尔多斯西南缘,可见奥陶纪重力流-海底扇沉积,既 沉积组合类型。九岭山南麓存在宜丰—南昌混杂岩 有碳酸盐岩重力流,也有陆源碎屑重力流。晚奥陶世 组合[7,78](需进一步认证),东乡—德兴—歙县含蓝到早石炭世,除鄂尔多斯西缘出现晚奥陶世和早石 片岩蛇绿混杂岩恢复为弧后洋盆俯冲消减的产物。 炭世沉积外,华北陆块区整体抬升上隆成剥蚀区,有 其南东侧怀玉火山弧被认为是发育在洋壳基础上, 可能是华北陆块与扬子陆块碰撞的后继效应。早石 怀玉基性火山岩以高硅高钛、低碱、拉斑玄武岩系列 炭世晚期古亚洲洋板块向华北陆块俯冲,在华北北 具洋壳亲缘性为特征,并与深水火山碎屑浊积岩系 缘发育了安第斯型活动边缘,其北缘转化为活动边 与碧玉岩共生[7],更可能是原华南大洋洋壳俯冲相 缘[56-58],大量闪长岩-石英闪长岩-花岗闪长岩侵位57,关的增生弧。宜丰九岭群火山岩为岛弧高铝玄武岩 并发育了一些岛弧火山岩[58],至二叠纪古亚洲洋最和拉斑玄武岩,显示江南九岭岩浆弧主体表现为近 终消亡。晚石炭世—早二叠世发育陆表海沉积,中二 东西向水下岛链环境[77],其南为万年岛弧。冷家溪群叠世到中三叠世为陆相沉积。 为鲍玛序列发育的富含凝灰质的砂泥质浊积岩,表 中—晚三叠世,受与古太平洋板块相互作用制 现为相对稳定的弧后盆地相沉积[79,80]。而江绍—萍约构造体制发现重大转折,进入东西分异、差异隆升 乡蛇绿混杂带中双溪坞群的岩石地球化学特征与洋 活动阶段。在华北北部发现的碱性—超基性岩、A型 内弧构造环境相似[81]。但最新的1:25万区调已将平花岗岩和闪长岩(225 Ma)中麻粒岩和堆晶岩包体,水组细碧角斑岩解体出来,并与共生的沉积地层一起 暗示华北早中生代已存在底侵作用[59]。中晚侏罗世建立了平水群,专指一套海相火山-沉积建造,而双溪 之交(161Ma前后)所谓的燕山运动的命名地,北京坞群则以章村组为代表,属陆缘弧环境的陆相火山- 西山髻山地区的构造运动和变形方式,不是造山事 沉积建造。
